Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is my eating at night. Most nights after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I’m eating way past a normal plate of food. Like, fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—if we had some event or family thing, whatever leftovers are there.

And I’ll just keep going until… I don’t know, sometimes a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to joke that I just like late-night snacks, like when we’d stay up in college and order food, but this isn’t really that. Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.

I used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Lately I’ve just… pulled back from all of that.

Um… I don’t know that I’d say I’m not aware of it. I mean, I’m awake enough to know I’m in the kitchen, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like sleepwalking or waking up with wrappers and no memory.

Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.

I used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. And lately I’ve pulled back from a lot of it. I know that’s probably not helpful.\n\nThere were moments where I felt low, like sitting there and realizing I’d been staring at the same thing for a while, but then I’d get distracted by some random errand or I’d start reorganizing something I didn’t need to reorganize. Like I found myself sorting old cables in a drawer the other day, and I don’t even know why I kept half of them.\n\nSo… days where it was most of the day? I can’t really say. I haven’t been keeping track that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. And lately I’ve pulled back from a lot of it. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, I guess. I feel drained, and I worry people can tell I’m not really keeping things together like I used to.\n\nIt’s weird, because those meetings aren’t even that dramatic—half the time it’s just arguing about folding chairs or who forgot to print flyers—but I’ll avoid going because I don’t want to be seen that way. So yeah, it has interfered some.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me more about how your nighttime eating specifically affects your ability to participate in social or community activities, and if you've noticed it impacting any other areas of your life like work or family responsibilities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve pulled back from community planning meetings and helping with events because I feel embarrassed, drained, and afraid people will notice I’m not holding things together the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it mostly shows up with the community stuff. I used to go to those planning meetings and help with little events, and now I just kind of… don’t. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or that they have enough people, but really I feel embarrassed.

After nights where I’ve eaten like that, I wake up feeling drained, and then I’m already thinking, “People are going to notice something’s off with me.” Like I’m not holding it together the way I used to.

And it’s not like those meetings are life-or-death. Sometimes they’re just talking about tables or permits or who’s bringing coffee, and somehow even that feels like too much. I start avoiding them. As far as work… I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say there. Family stuff, I don’t know, not as clearly. The community things are where I notice it the most.

Most nights after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I’m eating way past a normal plate of food. Like, fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—if we had some event or family thing, whatever leftovers are there.\n\nAnd I’ll just keep going until… I don’t know, sometimes a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge. It’s more like I get started and then suddenly there’s just a lot missing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating like this at night, do you feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s pretty much it. Like once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me. I’ll be standing there thinking, George, stop now, you don’t need more, and then somehow my hands are still making another plate or opening another container.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m confused, you know? I know what I’m doing. It’s just… I don’t feel like I can turn it off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of stress or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your relationships or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nI used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Lately I’ve just… pulled back from all of that.\n\nPart of it is I feel embarrassed, like people can tell I’m not really holding things together the way I used to. And I’m drained, too. Even just showing up and making small talk feels like a lot. So yeah, it’s not just eating and then forgetting about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up during the night feeling like you're not fully asleep and find yourself eating without really being aware of it, or do you feel unusually sleepy during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I’m not aware of it. My uncle used to fall asleep in his recliner every Sunday with the football game on, and everybody acted like that was normal. So I don’t really know where the line is.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. That part is probably worse than the tiredness, honestly.

After it happens, I just feel… sick with myself. Like, I’m sitting there thinking, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll think about how people see me—or how I want them to see me, I guess—as someone organized, dependable, strong. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.

It’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.

Most nights after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I’m eating way past a normal plate of food. Like, fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—if we had some event or family thing, whatever leftovers are there.\n\nAnd I’ll just keep going until… I don’t know, sometimes a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to joke that I just like late-night snacks, like when we’d stay up in college and order food, but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I get started and then suddenly there’s just a lot missing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating like this at night, do you feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s pretty much it. Like once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me. I’ll be standing there thinking, George, stop now, you don’t need more, and then somehow my hands are still making another plate or opening another container.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m confused, you know? I know what I’m doing. It’s just… I don’t feel like I can turn it off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of stress or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your relationships or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nI used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Even just showing up and making small talk feels like a lot. So yeah, it’s not just eating and then forgetting about it. I haven’t noticed anything like… unusual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like the community events you mentioned stepping back from?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. “Down” is kind of a big word, right? So maybe I lost patience more than interest.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as losing interest versus just not feeling up for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found yourself feeling down or depressed most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it that way. Like, most of the day, more days than not—that sounds very official, and I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I’m just in a mood, or I don’t want to talk to anybody, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like trying to fix the cabinet hinge in my kitchen for an hour even though I don’t know what I’m doing. description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision has already been made for me, and even while I’m telling myself, 'George, stop now,' my hands keep fixing another plate or opening another container.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I eat so fast during those times that I barely taste food I used to take pride in cooking, and sometimes I’m standing at the counter swallowing big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one.\n- key: S053\n  name: Eating_Without_Hunger\n  description: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n  manifestation: A lot of the time I’m not hungry at all—I may have just eaten dinner with my wife—but later I still go back and start picking at leftovers until it turns into another full-on spread.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel sick with myself, like I’m this man everybody sees as organized and strong, but I’m sitting there ashamed, guilty, and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: planning meetings and helping with events because I feel embarrassed, drained, and afraid people will notice I’m not holding things together the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the foreclosure process started, about a year and two months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. So yeah, it has interfered some.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your nighttime eating specifically affects your ability to participate in social or community activities, and if you've noticed it impacting any other areas of your life like work or family responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it mostly shows up with the community stuff. Sometimes they’re just talking about tables or permits or who’s bringing coffee, and somehow even that feels like too much. I start avoiding them. As far as work… I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say there. Family stuff, I don’t know, not as clearly. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re eating at night, do you ever feel like you’re only partly awake, or like you’re on autopilot, not fully in control of what you’re doing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say I’m half-asleep exactly. It’s more like… once I take that first bite, something clicks over, and then it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.

I can be standing there thinking, George, stop. Just stop now. And meanwhile my hands are still doing it—fixing another plate, opening another container, putting something else together like I’m just following instructions. It’s a strange feeling.

It reminds me of when you drive somewhere familiar and realize you don’t remember half the turns you made—not that it’s the same thing, but that kind of autopilot. Except with this, I’m aware enough to feel ashamed while it’s happening, which makes it worse.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is my eating at night. Most nights after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I’m eating way past a normal plate of food. Like, fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—if we had some event or family thing, whatever leftovers are there.\n\nAnd I’ll just keep going until… I don’t know, sometimes a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to joke that I just like late-night snacks, like when we’d stay up in college and order food, but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I get started and then suddenly there’s just a lot missing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating like this at night, do you feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s pretty much it. Like once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me. I’ll be standing there thinking, George, stop now, you don’t need more, and then somehow my hands are still making another plate or opening another container.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m confused, you know? I know what I’m doing. It’s just… I don’t feel like I can turn it off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of stress or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your relationships or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nI used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Lately I’ve just… pulled back from all of that.\n\nPart of it is I feel embarrassed, like people can tell I’m not really holding things together the way I used to. And I’m drained, too. Even just showing up and making small talk feels like a lot. So yeah, it’s not just eating and then forgetting about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up during the night feeling like you're not fully asleep and find yourself eating without really being aware of it, or do you feel unusually sleepy during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I’m not aware of it. I mean, I’m awake enough to know I’m in the kitchen, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like sleepwalking or waking up with wrappers and no memory.\n\nThe daytime sleepy part… I mean, I get tired, but who doesn’t, right? My uncle used to fall asleep in his recliner every Sunday with the football game on, and everybody acted like that was normal. So I don’t really know where the line is. I haven’t noticed anything like… unusual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like the community events you mentioned stepping back from?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. “Down” is kind of a big word, right? Like, everybody has days where they don’t want to deal with people.\n\nThe community stuff… yeah, I haven’t been going much, but part of that is just—well, those meetings can drag on forever. There was this one guy who’d argue for twenty minutes about where to put a trash can, and I’d sit there thinking, how is this my evening? So maybe I lost patience more than interest.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as losing interest versus just not feeling up for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found yourself feeling down or depressed most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it that way. Like, most of the day, more days than not—that sounds very official, and I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I’m just in a mood, or I don’t want to talk to anybody, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like trying to fix the cabinet hinge in my kitchen for an hour even though I don’t know what I’m doing. So I don’t know if that counts as depressed or just… being off.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it in that clear of a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a recent day when you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, but it’s hard to pin down. Like, I can remember days where I woke up already kind of annoyed or heavy, if that makes sense, but then the day gets chopped up by random stuff—phone calls, dishes, some YouTube thing I didn’t mean to watch for two hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “most of the day.” I don’t really keep track that way. Past few weeks… maybe a few off days? But I couldn’t honestly tell you how often without guessing.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there were any days in the past two weeks where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that happened?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Two weeks sounds short, but when I try to look back it all kind of blurs together.\n\nLike, I can remember being low in bits—standing in the kitchen or sitting on the couch and not wanting to move—but then I’ll get pulled into something else, or I’ll start messing with my phone, and suddenly it’s nighttime. So I don’t know if that means it was most of the day.\n\nIf I had to give you a number, I’d just be guessing. Maybe there were some days, but I can’t say how many.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past week where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that was?\n\nPatient: Past week… I don’t really know. I know that’s probably not helpful.\n\nThere were moments where I felt low, like sitting there and realizing I’d been staring at the same thing for a while, but then I’d get distracted by some random errand or I’d start reorganizing something I didn’t need to reorganize. Like I found myself sorting old cables in a drawer the other day, and I don’t even know why I kept half of them.\n\nSo… days where it was most of the day? I can’t really say. I haven’t been keeping track that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. Two weeks sounds short, but when I try to look back it all kind of blurs together.\n\nLike, I can remember being low in bits—standing in the kitchen or sitting on the couch and not wanting to move—but then I’ll get pulled into something else, or I’ll start messing with my phone, and suddenly it’s nighttime. So I don’t know if that means it was most of the day.\n\nIf I had to give you a number, I’d just be guessing. Maybe there were some days, but I can’t say how many.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past week where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that was?\n\nPatient: Past week… I don’t really know. I know that’s probably not helpful.\n\nThere were moments where I felt low, like sitting there and realizing I’d been staring at the same thing for a while, but then I’d get distracted by some random errand or I’d start reorganizing something I didn’t need to reorganize. Like I found myself sorting old cables in a drawer the other day, and I don’t even know why I kept half of them.\n\nSo… days where it was most of the day? I can’t really say. I haven’t been keeping track that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. Yeah… I guess it’s less with family directly and more that I’ve started avoiding people in general, especially the community planning stuff. Like, I used to show up and help with events, make calls, organize little things—nothing huge, but I was part of it.

Now after those nights, I feel embarrassed and wiped out, and I don’t want to be around people. I get this feeling like they’ll look at me and know I’m not holding things together the way I used to. Which is probably not true, I mean people are usually worried about their own lives—my neighbor once came to a whole meeting with two different shoes on and nobody said anything until afterward—but still, in my head it feels obvious.

So yeah, I’ve pulled back. I’ll make excuses or just not go. With family or friends, I don’t know if I can point to one big thing, but the avoiding, the not showing up… that’s definitely there.

Yeah… it has. I mean, I haven’t really sat people down and said, “This is what’s going on,” but it changes how I act around them.

I’ve pulled back from those community planning meetings and helping with events, which used to be something I actually liked doing. It’s more like I get started and then suddenly there’s just a lot missing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating like this at night, do you feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s pretty much it. I’ll be standing there thinking, George, stop now, you don’t need more, and then somehow my hands are still making another plate or opening another container.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m confused, you know? I know what I’m doing. It’s just… I don’t feel like I can turn it off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of stress or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your relationships or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nI used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Lately I’ve just… pulled back from all of that.\n\nPart of it is I feel embarrassed, like people can tell I’m not really holding things together the way I used to. And I’m drained, too. Even just showing up and making small talk feels like a lot. So yeah, it’s not just eating and then forgetting about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up during the night feeling like you're not fully asleep and find yourself eating without really being aware of it, or do you feel unusually sleepy during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I’m not aware of it. I mean, I’m awake enough to know I’m in the kitchen, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like sleepwalking or waking up with wrappers and no memory.\n\nThe daytime sleepy part… I mean, I get tired, but who doesn’t, right? My uncle used to fall asleep in his recliner every Sunday with the football game on, and everybody acted like that was normal. So I don’t really know where the line is. I haven’t noticed anything like… unusual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like the community events you mentioned stepping back from?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. “Down” is kind of a big word, right? Like, everybody has days where they don’t want to deal with people.\n\nThe community stuff… yeah, I haven’t been going much, but part of that is just—well, those meetings can drag on forever. There was this one guy who’d argue for twenty minutes about where to put a trash can, and I’d sit there thinking, how is this my evening? So maybe I lost patience more than interest.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as losing interest versus just not feeling up for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found yourself feeling down or depressed most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it that way. Like, most of the day, more days than not—that sounds very official, and I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I’m just in a mood, or I don’t want to talk to anybody, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like trying to fix the cabinet hinge in my kitchen for an hour even though I don’t know what I’m doing. So I don’t know if that counts as depressed or just… being off.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it in that clear of a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a recent day when you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, but it’s hard to pin down. Like, I can remember days where I woke up already kind of annoyed or heavy, if that makes sense, but then the day gets chopped up by random stuff—phone calls, dishes, some YouTube thing I didn’t mean to watch for two hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “most of the day.” I don’t really keep track that way. Past few weeks… maybe a few off days? But I couldn’t honestly tell you how often without guessing.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there were any days in the past two weeks where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that happened?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Two weeks sounds short, but when I try to look back it all kind of blurs together.\n\nLike, I can remember being low in bits—standing in the kitchen or sitting on the couch and not wanting to move—but then I’ll get pulled into something else, or I’ll start messing with my phone, and suddenly it’s nighttime. So I don’t know if that means it was most of the day.\n\nIf I had to give you a number, I’d just be guessing. Maybe there were some days, but I can’t say how many.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past week where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that was?\n\nPatient: Past week… I don’t really know. I know that’s probably not helpful.\n\nThere were moments where I felt low, like sitting there and realizing I’d been staring at the same thing for a while, but then I’d get distracted by some random errand or I’d start reorganizing something I didn’t need to reorganize. Like I found myself sorting old cables in a drawer the other day, and I don’t even know why I kept half of them.\n\nSo… days where it was most of the day? I can’t really say. I haven’t been keeping track that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. And lately I’ve pulled back from a lot of it. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, I guess. I feel drained, and I worry people can tell I’m not really keeping things together like I used to.\n\nIt’s weird, because those meetings aren’t even that dramatic—half the time it’s just arguing about folding chairs or who forgot to print flyers—but I’ll avoid going because I don’t want to be seen that way. So yeah, it has interfered some.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your nighttime eating specifically affects your ability to participate in social or community activities, and if you've noticed it impacting any other areas of your life like work or family responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it mostly shows up with the community stuff. I used to go to those planning meetings and help with little events, and now I just kind of… don’t. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or that they have enough people, but really I feel embarrassed.\n\nAfter nights where I’ve eaten like that, I wake up feeling drained, and then I’m already thinking, “People are going to notice something’s off with me.” Like I’m not holding it together the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s not like those meetings are life-or-death. Sometimes they’re just talking about tables or permits or who’s bringing coffee, and somehow even that feels like too much. I start avoiding them. As far as work… I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say there. Family stuff, I don’t know, not as clearly. The community things are where I notice it the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a lot of guilt, shame, or self-criticism specifically about the way you eat at night, even aside from how it affects your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part is probably worse than the tiredness, honestly.\n\nAfter it happens, I just feel… sick with myself. Like, I’m sitting there thinking, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll think about how people see me—or how I want them to see me, I guess—as someone organized, dependable, strong. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating at night, do you ever feel like you’re only partly awake, or like you’re on autopilot, not fully in control of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m half-asleep exactly. It’s more like… once I take that first bite, something clicks over, and then it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, George, stop. Just stop now. And meanwhile my hands are still doing it—fixing another plate, opening another container, putting something else together like I’m just following instructions. It’s a strange feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of when you drive somewhere familiar and realize you don’t remember half the turns you made—not that it’s the same thing, but that kind of autopilot. Except with this, I’m aware enough to feel ashamed while it’s happening, which makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Has your nighttime eating caused any problems in your personal relationships, or do you find yourself avoiding certain situations with your family or friends because of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s less with family directly and more that I’ve started avoiding people in general, especially the community planning stuff. Like, I used to show up and help with events, make calls, organize little things—nothing huge, but I was part of it.\n\nNow after those nights, I feel embarrassed and wiped out, and I don’t want to be around people. I get this feeling like they’ll look at me and know I’m not holding things together the way I used to. Which is probably not true, I mean people are usually worried about their own lives—my neighbor once came to a whole meeting with two different shoes on and nobody said anything until afterward—but still, in my head it feels obvious.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve pulled back. I’ll make excuses or just not go. With family or friends, I don’t know if I can point to one big thing, but the avoiding, the not showing up… that’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your nighttime eating has affected how you see yourself or caused problems in your personal relationships, even if it's not something you've talked about directly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I haven’t really sat people down and said, “This is what’s going on,” but it changes how I act around them.\n\nI’ve pulled back from those community planning meetings and helping with events, which used to be something I actually liked doing. After those nights I just feel embarrassed and drained, like I don’t want anyone looking too closely at me. It’s weird, because I know nobody’s probably thinking about me that much—people are busy worrying about parking permits and folding chairs and who forgot the sign-in sheet—but in my head it feels like they’ll notice I’m not holding things together like I used to.\n\nSo I just… don’t go sometimes. Or I make excuses. And then I feel worse about that too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F50.2", "F50.81"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I mean, I haven’t really sat people down and said, “This is what’s going on,” but it changes how I act around them.\n\nI’ve pulled back from those community planning meetings and helping with events, which used to be something I actually liked doing. After those nights I just feel embarrassed and drained, like I don’t want anyone looking too closely at me. It’s weird, because I know nobody’s probably thinking about me that much—people are busy worrying about parking permits and folding chairs and who forgot the sign-in sheet—but in my head it feels like they’ll notice I’m not holding things together like I used to.\n\nSo I just… don’t go sometimes. Or I make excuses. And then I feel worse about that too.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities, just don’t feel as good or interesting to you anymore?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t know if things don’t feel good anymore, or if I just don’t get around to them the way I used to.

Like, I’ll think about doing something, and then the day sort of gets away from me. Or I’ll sit down and there’s a rerun on TV, and then I remember this one actor was in something my mother used to watch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a clear thing, like “I don’t enjoy anything now.” It’s more hard for me to tell what’s lack of interest and what’s just me not following through.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.81\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is my eating at night. Most nights after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I’m eating way past a normal plate of food. Like, fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—if we had some event or family thing, whatever leftovers are there.\n\nAnd I’ll just keep going until… I don’t know, sometimes a whole tray is gone, or it feels like half the fridge. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to joke that I just like late-night snacks, like when we’d stay up in college and order food, but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I get started and then suddenly there’s just a lot missing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating like this at night, do you feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s pretty much it. Like once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision’s already been made for me. I’ll be standing there thinking, George, stop now, you don’t need more, and then somehow my hands are still making another plate or opening another container.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m confused, you know? I know what I’m doing. It’s just… I don’t feel like I can turn it off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of stress or interfere with your daily life, like affecting your relationships or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nI used to be pretty involved with, like, community planning stuff—meetings, helping set up events, making sure tables were where they needed to be, all that. I mean, I was never the mayor or anything, but people knew they could ask me to pitch in. Lately I’ve just… pulled back from all of that.\n\nPart of it is I feel embarrassed, like people can tell I’m not really holding things together the way I used to. And I’m drained, too. Even just showing up and making small talk feels like a lot. So yeah, it’s not just eating and then forgetting about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up during the night feeling like you're not fully asleep and find yourself eating without really being aware of it, or do you feel unusually sleepy during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I’m not aware of it. I mean, I’m awake enough to know I’m in the kitchen, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like sleepwalking or waking up with wrappers and no memory.\n\nThe daytime sleepy part… I mean, I get tired, but who doesn’t, right? My uncle used to fall asleep in his recliner every Sunday with the football game on, and everybody acted like that was normal. So I don’t really know where the line is. I haven’t noticed anything like… unusual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like the community events you mentioned stepping back from?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. “Down” is kind of a big word, right? Like, everybody has days where they don’t want to deal with people.\n\nThe community stuff… yeah, I haven’t been going much, but part of that is just—well, those meetings can drag on forever. There was this one guy who’d argue for twenty minutes about where to put a trash can, and I’d sit there thinking, how is this my evening? So maybe I lost patience more than interest.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as losing interest versus just not feeling up for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found yourself feeling down or depressed most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it that way. Like, most of the day, more days than not—that sounds very official, and I don’t really track it like that.\n\nSome days I’m just in a mood, or I don’t want to talk to anybody, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like trying to fix the cabinet hinge in my kitchen for an hour even though I don’t know what I’m doing. So I don’t know if that counts as depressed or just… being off.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it in that clear of a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a recent day when you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, but it’s hard to pin down. Like, I can remember days where I woke up already kind of annoyed or heavy, if that makes sense, but then the day gets chopped up by random stuff—phone calls, dishes, some YouTube thing I didn’t mean to watch for two hours.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “most of the day.” I don’t really keep track that way. Past few weeks… maybe a few off days? But I couldn’t honestly tell you how often without guessing.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there were any days in the past two weeks where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that happened?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Two weeks sounds short, but when I try to look back it all kind of blurs together.\n\nLike, I can remember being low in bits—standing in the kitchen or sitting on the couch and not wanting to move—but then I’ll get pulled into something else, or I’ll start messing with my phone, and suddenly it’s nighttime. So I don’t know if that means it was most of the day.\n\nIf I had to give you a number, I’d just be guessing. Maybe there were some days, but I can’t say how many.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past week where you felt down or low for most of the day, and if so, how many days would you say that was?\n\nPatient: Past week… I don’t really know. I know that’s probably not helpful.\n\nThere were moments where I felt low, like sitting there and realizing I’d been staring at the same thing for a while, but then I’d get distracted by some random errand or I’d start reorganizing something I didn’t need to reorganize. Like I found myself sorting old cables in a drawer the other day, and I don’t even know why I kept half of them.\n\nSo… days where it was most of the day? I can’t really say. I haven’t been keeping track that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so distressed about this nighttime eating that it interferes with your ability to function at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it does. Not like I can’t get out of bed or anything, but it sort of messes with how I show up.\n\nI used to be more involved with community planning meetings and helping set up events—like neighborhood stuff, little committees, things like that. And lately I’ve pulled back from a lot of it. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, I guess. I feel drained, and I worry people can tell I’m not really keeping things together like I used to.\n\nIt’s weird, because those meetings aren’t even that dramatic—half the time it’s just arguing about folding chairs or who forgot to print flyers—but I’ll avoid going because I don’t want to be seen that way. So yeah, it has interfered some.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your nighttime eating specifically affects your ability to participate in social or community activities, and if you've noticed it impacting any other areas of your life like work or family responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it mostly shows up with the community stuff. I used to go to those planning meetings and help with little events, and now I just kind of… don’t. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or that they have enough people, but really I feel embarrassed.\n\nAfter nights where I’ve eaten like that, I wake up feeling drained, and then I’m already thinking, “People are going to notice something’s off with me.” Like I’m not holding it together the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s not like those meetings are life-or-death. Sometimes they’re just talking about tables or permits or who’s bringing coffee, and somehow even that feels like too much. I start avoiding them. As far as work… I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say there. Family stuff, I don’t know, not as clearly. The community things are where I notice it the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a lot of guilt, shame, or self-criticism specifically about the way you eat at night, even aside from how it affects your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part is probably worse than the tiredness, honestly.\n\nAfter it happens, I just feel… sick with myself. Like, I’m sitting there thinking, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll think about how people see me—or how I want them to see me, I guess—as someone organized, dependable, strong. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating at night, do you ever feel like you’re only partly awake, or like you’re on autopilot, not fully in control of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m half-asleep exactly. It’s more like… once I take that first bite, something clicks over, and then it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, George, stop. Just stop now. So yeah, it has interfered some.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your nighttime eating specifically affects your ability to participate in social or community activities, and if you've noticed it impacting any other areas of your life like work or family responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it mostly shows up with the community stuff. I used to go to those planning meetings and help with little events, and now I just kind of… don’t. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or that they have enough people, but really I feel embarrassed.\n\nAfter nights where I’ve eaten like that, I wake up feeling drained, and then I’m already thinking, “People are going to notice something’s off with me.” Like I’m not holding it together the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s not like those meetings are life-or-death. Sometimes they’re just talking about tables or permits or who’s bringing coffee, and somehow even that feels like too much. I start avoiding them. As far as work… I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say there. Family stuff, I don’t know, not as clearly. The community things are where I notice it the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a lot of guilt, shame, or self-criticism specifically about the way you eat at night, even aside from how it affects your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That part is probably worse than the tiredness, honestly.\n\nAfter it happens, I just feel… sick with myself. Like, I’m sitting there thinking, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll think about how people see me—or how I want them to see me, I guess—as someone organized, dependable, strong. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating at night, do you ever feel like you’re only partly awake, or like you’re on autopilot, not fully in control of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m half-asleep exactly. It’s more like… once I take that first bite, something clicks over, and then it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, George, stop. Just stop now. And meanwhile my hands are still doing it—fixing another plate, opening another container, putting something else together like I’m just following instructions. It’s a strange feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of when you drive somewhere familiar and realize you don’t remember half the turns you made—not that it’s the same thing, but that kind of autopilot. Except with this, I’m aware enough to feel ashamed while it’s happening, which makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Has your nighttime eating caused any problems in your personal relationships, or do you find yourself avoiding certain situations with your family or friends because of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s less with family directly and more that I’ve started avoiding people in general, especially the community planning stuff. The guy who can handle things, who keeps the meeting notes straight, who knows where the extra extension cords are… and then there I am, alone at night, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, I ate too much.” It feels like I failed at being the person I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating at night, do you ever feel like you’re only partly awake, or like you’re on autopilot, not fully in control of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m half-asleep exactly. It’s more like… once I take that first bite, something clicks over, and then it feels like the decision’s already been made for me.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, George, stop. Just stop now. And meanwhile my hands are still doing it—fixing another plate, opening another container, putting something else together like I’m just following instructions. It’s a strange feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of when you drive somewhere familiar and realize you don’t remember half the turns you made—not that it’s the same thing, but that kind of autopilot. Except with this, I’m aware enough to feel ashamed while it’s happening, which makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Has your nighttime eating caused any problems in your personal relationships, or do you find yourself avoiding certain situations with your family or friends because of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it’s less with family directly and more that I’ve started avoiding people in general, especially the community planning stuff. Like, I used to show up and help with events, make calls, organize little things—nothing huge, but I was part of it.\n\nNow after those nights, I feel embarrassed and wiped out, and I don’t want to be around people. I get this feeling like they’ll look at me and know I’m not holding things together the way I used to. Which is probably not true, I mean people are usually worried about their own lives—my neighbor once came to a whole meeting with two different shoes on and nobody said anything until afterward—but still, in my head it feels obvious.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve pulled back. I’ll make excuses or just not go. With family or friends, I don’t know if I can point to one big thing, but the avoiding, the not showing up… that’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your nighttime eating has affected how you see yourself or caused problems in your personal relationships, even if it's not something you've talked about directly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I haven’t really sat people down and said, “This is what’s going on,” but it changes how I act around them.\n\nI’ve pulled back from those community planning meetings and helping with events, which used to be something I actually liked doing. After those nights I just feel embarrassed and drained, like I don’t want anyone looking too closely at me. It’s weird, because I know nobody’s probably thinking about me that much—people are busy worrying about parking permits and folding chairs and who forgot the sign-in sheet—but in my head it feels like they’ll notice I’m not holding things together like I used to.\n\nSo I just… don’t go sometimes. Or I make excuses. And then I feel worse about that too.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities, just don’t feel as good or interesting to you anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t know if things don’t feel good anymore, or if I just don’t get around to them the way I used to.\n\nLike, I’ll think about doing something, and then the day sort of gets away from me. Or I’ll sit down and there’s a rerun on TV, and then I remember this one actor was in something my mother used to watch—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a clear thing, like “I don’t enjoy anything now.” It’s more hard for me to tell what’s lack of interest and what’s just me not following through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to really enjoy, like hobbies or activities, don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you anymore—even if you still do them sometimes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’m having trouble separating whether the things themselves feel less rewarding, or if I just… don’t make the effort to do them.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll still watch something or think about doing an activity, and it’s okay, but then I get distracted or I don’t follow through. And then I start thinking about, I don’t know, how I used to be better at keeping a routine—like even stupid little routines, watering a plant on the same day or remembering when the recycling goes out. Not that that’s a hobby, obviously.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say they don’t feel interesting anymore. I haven’t noticed it that clearly. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
